| **[FaultGene: LLM-Assisted Fault-Augmented Test Generation for Hardware-in-the-Loop Validation of Autonomous Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29969v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable validation of autonomous driving cyber-physical systems requires testing not only nominal driving behavior but also failures arising from faults in sensing and embedded computation. | **[Commit or Abstain? | **[Privacy in Context Is Being Overlooked in the Age of General-Purpose LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29925v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) continue to grow in scope and capability, privacy concerns are on the rise. Furthermore, we argue that even future work in this direction needs to wrestle with fundamental tensions across various desirable properties of prompt privacy. We recommend three critical paths forward: application-specific deployment of LLMs, context-specific evaluation of privacy frameworks, and better privacy literacy.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at Personalization in the Era of Large Foundation Models Workshop @ AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[Unintended Side Effects of Value Training in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29922v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLMs are increasingly deployed in value-sensitive contexts, aligning them with human values has emerged as a post-training objective in NLP, aimed at instilling additional values and guiding model behaviour. | **[Asking a Multimodal LLM Whether Someone Fell: Zero-Shot Screening of Low-Resolution Thermal Video in a Laboratory and an Occupied Home](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29913v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Thermal cameras can watch over older people living alone without capturing faces, and multimodal large language models (LLMs) can judge a short video without task-specific training. | **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. | **[HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29873v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. As a result, AI accelerators achieve varying levels of efficiency across the two phases. GPUs are well suited to prefill, but are often underutilized during decode. By contrast, NPUs can utilize their compute resources more effectively during decode. Motivated by this observation, we explore a heterogeneous architecture that integrates GPUs and NPUs, mapping prefill to GPUs and decode to NPUs to achieve high performance and efficiency. However, realizing such heterogeneous integration is challenging because LLM phase disaggregation requires KV cache transfer from prefill to decode (that is, from GPUs to NPUs), which is complicated by differences in inter-device communication mechanisms and KV cache tensor layouts. To address this, we propose HAWK, a transfer mechanism that enables rapid KV cache transfer across heterogeneous devices. HAWK establishes a remote direct memory access (RDMA) path for direct cross-device transfers while handling inter-device tensor layout discrepancies as part of the transfer process. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. By aligning generative likelihoods with semantic correctness, this work establishes a framework for deploying trustworthy LLMs in complex, open-ended applications. | **[UlaCache: Unified Logical Addressing for Sparse LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29820v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long contexts increase the memory cost of the key-value (KV) cache in large language model (LLM) inference. | **[Neither Stochastic Parroting nor AGI: LLMs Solve Tasks through Context-Directed Extrapolation from Training Data Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23323v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This position paper argues that many behaviours framed as either "stochastic parroting" or "advanced reasoning" are better explained as context-directed extrapolation from training-data priors, and that ML and NLP research communities would benefit from evaluations that distinguish remembering and basic extrapolation from genuine application-level generalisation. | **[Where Do LLMs Learn to Recommend AI?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29785v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Asked what to study, which career to pursue, or what product to build, large language models (LLMs) often recommend AI-related options. | **[BrickWright: Agentic Creation of Physically Feasible Brick Builds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31651v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier coding agents are becoming increasingly adept at 3D modeling. Yet, they still face challenges when building objects meant to be assembled in the physical world, often creating invalid assemblies with floating, colliding, or disconnected parts. We argue that providing agents with tools for assembling builds and verifying their physical feasibility is sufficient for them to produce physically valid assemblies that still adhere to input prompts. Focusing on the domain of LEGO brick builds, we present BrickWright, a framework for generating physically feasible brick builds from text or image prompts using pre-trained agents, where a build is physically feasible if it uses only real parts, has no colliding or floating parts, and forms a connected structure if possible. BrickWright guides agents to create builds by writing code with BrickLayer, a Python toolkit with two main components: an ergonomic brick assembly API that halves inference cost compared to working in raw LDraw, and a validator that provides feedback on physical feasibility. Whereas baseline agents achieve physical validity rates ranging from only 9% (GPT-6 Luna) to 69% (GPT-6 Astra), BrickWright-guided agents achieve 100% validity on a sample of 512 prompts, while improving prompt adherence for lightweight agents and maintaining it for stronger agents. Additionally, they outperform specialized models on the text-to-brick task. Code is available on our project page: https://avalovelace1.github.io/brickwright/</p></details> |  |
| **[EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31650v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATTUNE: Agentic Throughput Tuning for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17528v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The throughput of an LLM training job depends on its recipe: how the model is split across GPUs, how memory is traded for compute, and which kernels and communication overlaps it uses. The best recipe changes with every model, accelerator and framework version. Automated tuners search a fixed space of knobs and check only that a job runs, so they miss levers outside that space and can ship a configuration that silently changes training. ATTUNE is a coding agent that tunes any working recipe unattended within a GPU-hour budget. Guided by a skill of search rules, scripts and lessons from past tuning campaigns, it diagnoses each run and changes one lever at a time. A correctness gate outside the agent refuses any argument known to change the training objective. ATTUNE tunes Amazon's production post-training recipes on H100, H200 and B200 clusters, raising throughput by 1.7x on average. It beats the published throughput of four of NVIDIA's own benchmark recipes on NVIDIA's AutoModel framework by 6-17%. On models held out from its development, it improves the strongest public recipes most where they were written for another setting, by up to 2.6x on MiniMax-M2, and least where the vendor tuned them for the same hardware. Given the same GPU-hour budget, it outperforms existing automated tuners, including Bayesian optimization, random search and OptiCo, a recent LLM-agent tuner. Its gain on GLM-4.5 is more than four times the best alternative's. The same coding agent without ATTUNE's skill gains less and fails half its launches. We release ATTUNE at https://github.com/amazon-science/attune and OpenRecipes, a leaderboard of tuned and verified training recipes, at https://openrecipes.ai.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures. v2: adds NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel benchmark recipes, production deployment results and the OpenRecipes leaderboard</p></details> |
| **[Can an Agent Debug a Web Corpus? Agentic Auditing of Slovak FineWeb2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31636v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multilingual pipelines such as FineWeb2 apply one recipe across a thousand languages, leaving the failure modes of individual languages unanticipated. On the 26.5M-document Slovak portion of FineWeb2, three runs costing \$2.21-\$2.84 each found seven findings over four phenomena. | **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. | **[Same Luck, Different Choice: Twin-World Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31544v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic reinforcement learning rewards a complete episode, yet the policy needs credit for every decision inside it. Twin credit is unbiased for any coupling quality, its variance is that of an independent counterfactual scaled by $1-ρ$, where $ρ$ is the twins' outcome correlation, and under coupling the number of rollouts needed to resolve an advantage of size $Δ$ falls from $Θ(Δ^{-2})$ to $Θ(Δ^{-1})$. | **[AgenticEvolve: Evolutionary Program Search with Persistent Language Model Agents that Design and Run Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31326v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evolutionary program search with large language models keeps the structure of an evolutionary algorithm and uses the model as its variation operator. On the write path, \textsc{Saria} uses eviction and reuse history to selectively persist KV cache blocks that exhibit reuse opportunities beyond their DRAM residency, avoiding unnecessary writes for KV cache already hosted in DRAM. On the read path, \textsc{Saria} promotes selected SSD-hit KV cache blocks from GPU memory into DRAM after prefill, allowing subsequent reuse within the same session to reduce SSD read traffic while limiting DRAM contention. A transfer coordinator paces DRAM reads while SSD transfers are active, allowing both paths to make progress and efficiently share GPU ingress bandwidth. | **[From Faulty Memories to Corrected Actions: Dependency-Guided Rollback Repair for Memory-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10502v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persistent memory lets language model agents reuse information across sessions, but it also makes errors durable: a poisoned, stale, or misattributed record can alter reasoning, tool use, answers, and subsequent memory writes. Existing defenses mainly detect or delete suspicious memories, or revise the current response. Deleting the source leaves already propagated claims, actions, and derived memories active, whereas resetting the store or replaying the full trace destroys benign state and repeats unnecessary computation. We therefore formulate post-failure memory recovery: given a failed execution and diagnosed faulty memories, regenerate the answer and clean up affected persistent state while retaining unaffected work. Our dependency-guided rollback repair builds a typed memory-to-action graph from runtime provenance, traces explicit downstream dependencies, preserves candidates with independent trusted support, deactivates unsupported memory state, and selectively replays only answer-relevant affected computation. We evaluate this approach on a 150-case controlled benchmark spanning three tool-use domains and four memory failure types, and on a 50-case trajectory-derived stress test adapted from LongMemEval-V2. On the controlled benchmark, it achieves 85.3% immediate recovery versus 77.3% for LLM-judge repair, the strongest baseline, while preserving all benign memories and using 41.8% fewer LLM calls. Selective replay, however, leaves higher recurrence than LLM-judge repair (26.6% vs. 12.1%), exposing a trade-off between targeted repair and broader recomputation. On the adapted subset, it reaches 68.0% recovery versus 54.0% for the next best method, with 5.9% recurrence. Code and data are available at https://github.com/xiaoqi-7/memory-rollback.</p></details> |  |
| **[Know When to Escalate: Critic-Guided Adaptive Execution for Cloud-Local Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30587v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in foundation-model agents have enabled increasingly capable embodied systems for long-horizon planning and interaction. However, embodied agents require fast, closed-loop decisions as they continuously perceive and act on a changing environment. Deploying strong cloud agents leaves the platform idle for seconds between consecutive actions, while lightweight local models respond quickly but are more vulnerable to errors. Existing hierarchical architectures typically assign models fixed roles, whereas conventional model routers make stateless per-request decisions. We present CARE, a critic-guided adaptive execution framework that dynamically determines when to escalate control from a local executor to a stronger cloud model. A lightweight critic model monitors execution reliability and triggers escalation when local execution becomes unreliable; once escalated, control persists until the agent recovers, avoiding step-wise switching. To support such control transfer, a shared handoff memory maintains execution context and task-level progress across executors, enabling the model to intervene without restarting from scratch. Across diverse embodied benchmarks, CARE improves task success by 6.3-18.3 percentage points over the strongest evaluated collaboration baseline. CARE further approaches or exceeds Cloud-only success while reducing median time-between-action latency by 28.8-82.6%.</p></details> |  |
| **[DagFlow AI: Agentic Synthetic Data Generation for Personalized Statistics Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30580v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To provide personalized learning experiences in courses that teach data analysis skills, instructors often search for datasets that appeal to a student's interests, align with specific learning objectives, and are appropriate for the student's skill level. Datasets that match all three criteria can be difficult to find. DagFlow-AI is an agentic AI data generation tool that creates realistic, synthetic datasets tailored to these criteria. Rather than mimicking existing datasets or generating observations directly, DagFlow-AI uses the broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to specify the components of transparent, reproducible data-generating mechanisms from which observations are generated programmatically. We illustrate how the tool can be used to construct a realistic, synthetic dataset and describe how it aligns with both the Guidelines for Assessment and Instruction of Statistics Education (GAISE) and the Problem, Plan, Data, Analysis, and Conclusion (PPDAC) framework for teaching data analysis</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, submitted to Technological Innovation in Statistics Education</p></details> |
| **[Diagnosing Personalization Failures in Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30573v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unlike conventional GUI agents, personalized GUI agents require user preferences to guide sequential execution over changing GUI states. This raises two challenges: determining HOW each preference should alter execution strategy and WHEN it should take effect along the interaction trajectory. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate overall task success, offering limited insight into these questions. We introduce PrefDiag-Bench with two components: (1) a categorization of preferences into five types, characterizing HOW they affect execution; and (2) a stage-wise diagnostic framework with 1,664 probes, tracing WHEN preferences should take effect and where failures occur. We evaluate eleven GUI agent systems, including end-to-end agents and agentic workflows, under two forms of user context: explicit user profiles and behavioral memory logs. Although existing agents achieve relatively high completion rates on non-personalized tasks, performance is substantially lower on preference-personalized tasks. Even the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves only 47.17% on Process preferences under explicit user profiles. Agents struggle particularly with Ranking and Process preferences. Further analysis reveals two key capability bottlenecks: selecting relevant evidence from user history and recognizing when preferences should take effect during execution. Controlled interventions that explicitly provide relevant evidence improve end-to-end task success by up to 7.06%. PrefDiag-Bench offers a more systematic evaluation of GUI agents' personalization capabilities beyond overall task success. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Zhixin-L/PrefDiag-Bench.</p></details> | 24 pages, 8 figures |
| **[Stale Instruction Files Override the Code in Coding Agents: A One-Sentence Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30571v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Purpose: Coding agents follow a project's instruction file, such as CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md, even when it disagrees with the existing code and even when they notice the disagreement. We measure this behaviour and test whether one sentence in the user's own instruction file makes agents follow the code instead. Methods: We built 40 Python projects in which the instruction file asks for one convention while the existing code uses another, for example print instead of logging. Claude Code and Codex CLI added a feature to each project, with and without a sentence in the user-level instruction file that gives the existing code precedence, in 320 runs. Results: Without the sentence, 90.1% of the agents' choices followed the stale file. Claude Code pointed out the conflict in 79 of 80 runs and still followed the file. Codex CLI rewrote the existing code to match the file in 20 of 80 runs, and the project's own tests then failed in 17 of them. The sentence raised the share of choices that follow the code from 4.2% to 91.4%, stopped the rewrites and raised the test pass rate from 89.4% to 100%. Conclusion: We attribute the behaviour to how agents receive the two sources: the instruction file reaches the model as an instruction and the code as tool output, which models are trained to rank lower. Users can add the sentence today, and makers of agents and models can build the same rule into their products.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 8 figures. Submitted to SN Computer Science. Data and code: https://github.com/WenJing95/stale-docs-data</p></details> |
| **[Governed Self-Evolution for Autonomous Investing: A Multi-Agent LLM System with an Invariant Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30557v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that act on live Web data must combine heterogeneous evidence, remember what they have done and improve from outcomes without weakening their own safeguards. We study this problem in autonomous investing, where every action is irreversible and based on untrusted Web content. We present Steward, a multi-agent LLM system that draws the boundary of self-evolution in its type system. First, nine role-specialised agents decide jointly: four analysts read news feeds, SEC filings, macroeconomic series and market data and emit typed reports, from which a Portfolio Manager sets target positions that a protected Risk Manager audits. Second, a seven-layer shared memory, assembled by code from the system's own record, gives each decision its position context, the market narrative, calibration and the agent's past decisions. Third, the system improves itself by review: code grades every trade and forecast nightly and writes the grades into memory, and each quarter a reflector proposes prompt learnings that a deterministic gate applies reversibly and replay of recorded inputs verifies. All three operate around an invariant core of order arithmetic, risk rules and discipline-agent prompts that no self-edit can reach. In a five-month live deployment (102 trading days, 1,632 LLM calls), the system contained a 24.6% sector drawdown to a 0.3% loss; in replay of its recorded inputs, a single model given the same raw data traded more and lost 1.35 points against the market over five days where the joint design showed no significant loss, and no injected instruction was followed; and a 2015-2026 backtest shows the core alone lowering the maximum drawdown under all 50 random entry policies. The reflector traced the dominant failure to its root cause, and the gate applied a learning that the agent adopted on trigger evenings and on no control evening. Code: https://github.com/yebof/quant-agent</p></details> |  |
| **[Atlas: Making Sense of Long Horizon Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30548v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents can now search literature, generate hypotheses, analyze datasets, and run experiments at a scale difficult for researchers to match. Yet scaling scientific execution does not automatically scale researchers' ability to make sense of what agents produce. When researchers return to a long-horizon run, they must evaluate work they did not monitor: what the agent produced, which decisions shaped those outputs, and whether those decisions served the research goal. We frame this post-run sensemaking as a representation problem across three linked levels: goal, decision, and artifact. From this framework we developed Atlas, an agentic workspace with a trajectory tree, which renders the run as a branching structure showing what the agent chose and set aside, and artifact projections over six representations. In a within-subjects study (N=14), participants reported higher decision transparency, artifact sensemaking, and goal connection with Atlas than with a standard baseline, together with substantially lower subjective workload.</p></details> |  |
